xefuk
July 25, 1948.
MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES
Mr. Corkran called Captain Rawls today regarding the
case of Mrs. de Villiers, who accompan1ed her husband from
South Africa to Singapore under the assumption that her
clearance to enter Japan would be available. Captain Rawls
said that SCAP has asked for more detailed information as
to why Mrs. de Villiers finds it necessary to enter Japan
for business and until she supplies that information her
894.
application cannot be presented. He suggested that we
obtain from her the name of her company, the nature of its
business in Japan, and why her presence in Japan is necessary
in addition to that of her husband. He added that we should
inform her that her application will require at least two
weeks to be acted upon and that she should not expect to
enter Japan before that time.
7-2348
DOT Gen 
Dis
CSA
ode
DS:HCorkran:erk

July 23, 1948.
MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES
A military permit to enter Japan was received today
for Mr. Bernard Jacob Etzin. To it was attached a note
saying that the permit is to be given to Mr. Etzin only
with the understanding that he will have a permit for re-
entry into the United States or entrance into another
country upon the conclusion of his 60-day visit in Japan.
a
Upon receipt of Mr. Etzin's permit, Mr. Corkran called
Mrs. Dayton in the Immigration and Naturalization Service.
Mrs. Dayton asked for certain pertinent information regard-
ing Mr. Etzin's military permit, and then she said that his
re-entry permit would be mailed to him today.

Dairen, China, July 25, 1948
UNCLASSIFTED
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Subject: Repatriation of Najiy and Anisa KURBANGALI
from Dairen.
Yill
Reo'd
The United States Political Adviser
August 23
to Supreme Commander for Allied Powers,
894.
ACTION
FE
Tokyo.
DS
Sir:
INFO
DCR
I have the honor to refer to Consulate telegrams
7
11/7-2548
6 and 7, July 10, 1948 and SCAP telegram CX 62335,
July 17. These telegrams referred to the request of
Najiy end Anisa KURBANGALI and their aunt Mahisarwary
ANGURAZI for permission to enter Japan and to the
granting of that permission by SCAP.
The Soviet authorities at Dairen had promised this
family they could leave if they received SCAP clearance.
Once this was reoeived, however, the authorities refused
to let them go on the grounds they had no passports. The
details regarding their lack of passports had been sup-
plied to the Soviets at the time of the ir original appli-
cation. Hence, it is possible this may not be the real
reason for their being held at Dairen.
The tvo girls say they were born in Japan and have
never been outside Japanese territory. Therefore, they
have had no need for passports. They came to Dairen
shortly before the war. Their parents are White Russians
of Tartar extraction.
Since there are no postal connections out of Dairen,
the Consulate has agreed to transmit their passport photo-
CS/
graphs to the American authorities at Tokyo. If no objeo-
tion is perceived it is requested that the photographs
and the attaohed letter be forwarded to the girls' family
who then, presumably, will be able to take up with the
appropriate authorities the issuanoe of passports for
them. The 1945 address of the family was 1098 Shiburaku
Yoyogi Uehara. Tokyo. Their mother's namesis Emma KURBAN
GALI and their grandmother's name is Haniva ANGURAZI.
It is also requested that this office be notified
whether this is still the correct address of the family.
Previous

Reference is made to your memorandum of 13 July 1948.
file
fe
Paragraph checked (x) indicates action on subject.
(x ) The individuals listed below have been cleared by the military author-
ities to enter
Japan
for 60 days after date of entry. Two
standard sized passport photographs will be carried by individuals for identifi-
cation purposes in order to enter. Individual's name; firm, when applicable;
location, when applicable; and entry periods may be found below.
( ) If entering Korea transportation vill be at no cost to the United
States Government. Apnlicant is authorized to ship personal baggage and commer-
cial samples only, .subject to United States Department of Commerce Regulations

Guatemala, August 9, 116

SUBJECT:
Editorial commending United Fruit
Company for promoting tourist travel
INFO
to Guatemala.
DCR
DVISION OF
ARA
TRC
THE LONORABLE
PROTECTIVE SERVICES
EUR
THE SECRETARY OF STATE,
ITP
POS
OLA
WASHINGTOND.C.
DN OF STATE
FC
894
CIA
SIR:
Referring to the Embassy despatch no. 38o of
July 29 with regard to the publication in the local
Xπ
press of allegations emanating from a "well informed
person within the Government" that certain foreign
transportation companies are conducting an intense
propaganda campaign in the United States to discourage
814.
tourist travel to Guatemala, I have the honor to re-
port that the leading newspaper, El Imparcial. took.
-948
cognizance of these rumors in a helpful editorial on
August 7. warmly praising the United Fruit Company for
1ts effective propaganda in encouraging tourism in
Guatemala.
Apropos these charges against certain foreign
transportation companies, El Imparcial observes that
there has come to its attention a well-illuatrated.
attractive folder advertising Guatemalen tourist
attractions, whioh the United Fruit Company has
publiahed and distributed widely in the United States.
Moreover, the article notes, statistics prove that
the Groat White Fleet, which the Company maintains in
passengor service, brought 2, 395 tourists to Guatemala
in 1947. Admitting that the United Fruit Company
makos a business of this passenger steamship servioe,
it must be admitted, also, that the service results
in substantial bonofit to Guatemala.
The oditorial implies that current rumors against
foreign tranaportation companies are part of the
effort being made to persuade Congress of the desir-
ability of appropriating funds to continue the
National Tourist Office (funds for whioh were eliminated
from the l949 budget), and adds that El Imparoial dees
not wish to question the effeotivenees of this Govern-
mental agency, only to draw attention to a fine
oxample of United Fruit Company's effeotive efferts in
promoting tourism.
Respeotfully yours.
For the Ambassador
Milton K. Wells

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
SEKAX DIVISION OF
PROTECTIVE SERVICES
MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES
On Monday, August 9 Captain Rawls of the Military Permit
Section, Civil Affairs Division, Army Department called DS and
stated that through error he had sent the Military Permit for
Thomas Ellis Carr to enter Okinawa to the Passport Division
instead of to DS. Mr. Carr is now at Hong Kong. Mrs. Mellor
called Mrs. Simpson who stated she had received the permit
nd had sent it to DS with an urgent tag on the envelope. PD
and DS have each received calls from Mr. Bogatko in New York
regarding Mr. Carr's military permit. Mr. Bogatko has been
informed that the permit was sent to the wrong section and
could not reach DS before this morning. He is again calling
the Department at 1l:30 to ascertain if the telegram to
Hong Kong has been sent. The information in the telegram
attached was given to Mrs. Mellor over the telephone this
morning by Captain Rawls, who is anxious that it be despatched
today.
SEP 17 1948
FILED
DS:ISMellor:MMcC

330
Reference is made to the Embassy's airgram A-195
of August 13, 1948 and to the Department's telegram
no. 752 of August 6, 1948 regarding the statement in
English to be placed in the passport of an individual
who has been grented permission to enter Japan.
While the strict interpretation of paragraph 9 SCAP
8
Circular 8 of August 30, 1948 would require that extansive
information be placed in a prospective visitor's passport
二
in practice a simple statement in English that the
3
individual has been cleared by the military authorities in
Japan to enter Japan between given dates for a stay of sixty
days after his date of entry, is all that is required. This
is no more than is provided the traveler by means of
certified copies of Department telegrams transmitting
military clearance when such are used. It is easy for the
traveler himself to supplement the statement of clearance
in either case by carrying with his passport copies of his
original application papers which must have included the
information required by paragraph 7b. of SCAP Circular 8.
The procedure of using certified copies of Department
telegrams arose to meet the needs of travelers who were
stateless or who had no representative of their own govern-
ment near them. Thus it has been uncommon for certified
copies to be issued to travelers starting their trips
from their own capital cities. No objection is seen to the
continuance of the practice by your office,however,provided
it does not prove burdensome to the staff.
C
S
If such work is burdeneome it is suggested that the
A1
DISTRIBUTION
Embassy set the entire circular 8 procedure back into the
DESIRED
originally intended channel See FSs 667 January 17,19477
(OFFICES ONLY)
wherein the applicant goes to his own Foreign Office which
transmits the application to its Embassy at Washington
which sends it to the Department which transmits it to the
Department of the Army for forwarding to Japan. The SCAP
answer goes through the same steps in reverse and the local
American mission accordingly does not participate in the
matter atall.
In short
CR CLEARANCE
UNCIASSIFIED
☆ U. G. GOVERNMENY PRINTING OFFICE - 194S 784225

-2
In short, it is desirable whenever feasible that
clearance information be inserted by the Foreign Office
in the passport of the individual who has been cleared
for entry into Japan; otherwise, you may issue a
certified copy of the Department's telegram stating
that military clearance has been received from SCAP.

We have to request that necessary permission
may kindly be sought from the SUPREME COMMAND ALLIED POWERS
for U Thein, Managing Director of Maung Thein Bros. Ltd., and
U Hla Thwin, his personal Secretary, to visit JAPAN.
The purpose of the visit is to exchange information
on trading conditions and to make necessary business negotia
tions with the firm of NICHIMEN JITSUGYO KABUSHIKI KAISHA
of SAKA, JAPAN. We have been in correspondence with this
firm for a long time past and recently we received a telegram
from them inviting us to visit them immediately. This firm is
our very old clients and we had had considerable business with
them both in pre-war years and during the Japanese Occupation
of Burma.
Ve now desire to resume our business relations and
to negotiate with them for the exchange of commodities as soon
as free trade is permitted. Our visit will help to promote
our future business to a great extent.
We also intend to make short sightseeing tours
to places of interest and scenic beauty within Japan.
Necessary individual particulars of U Thein and
U Hla Thwin are submitted herewith. A testimonial from the
Secretary, Burma Chamber of Commerce & Industry is also
enclosed.
Thanking you, we remain,

RANGOON.
On August 16, 1948 the Embassy of Burma in Washington
addressed a note to the Department under cover of which it
894
transmitted the applications or U. Thein and U. Hla Thwin,
Managing Director and Assistant Manager, respectively, of
Maung Thwein Brothers, Limited, Rangoon for permission to
enter Japan. There was attached to the Embassy's note under
reference a copy of a note from the Permanent Seoretary of
the Foreign Office to the Embassy of Burma in Washington
1/8-1648
requesting that the Department of State be approached for
the necessary permission for the aforementioned individuals
to enter Japan. It 1s further stated in the copy of the note
from the Foreign Office that "it is understood that the
American Embassy at Rangoon advised the applioants to make
an application to the Department of State.
For your information and future guidance the Military
authorities in the Department of the Army have recently
informed the Department that all applications to enter Japan
from persons whose Government's interests are protected in
Japan by a third Power should now be submitted through the
facilities of the protecting Power, except in extremely unusual
circumstances, which would render such handling impractical.
In as much as the interests of the Burmese Government in Japan
are under the protection of the United Kingdom the Department
has replied to the Embassy of Burma in the sense of the
foregoing and has requested the Embassy to inform its Govern-
ment that the applications of Messrs. Thein and Thwin should
be submitted to the Supreme Commander through British
CS
channels.

=== Page 121 === (confidence 0.95)

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
SPEXXKA0IX0YXX0IO0KK
DIVISION OF PROTECTIVE SERVICES
14/R
MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES
The Department, in its note of July 1, 1948 to the Charge
d'Affaires ad interim of the Argentine Republic, referred to
FW
previous correspondence and again mentioned the difficulties
encountered by its representatives in Japan in their endeavor
894.111/8-1648
to perforn requested services on behalf of the Argentine Govern-
ment. A request was made for clarification of the question of
Argentine representation in Japan, reported to be formally
entrusted to the Swedish Mission at Tokyo. It was stated that
action with regard to the Embassy's request of May 10 involving
the repatriation of 28 persons would be postponed until a reply
was received from the Embassy regarding the suggestions contained
in the Department's previous note of March 16.
On August 26, 1948, the Department stated in its note to
the Argentine Ambassador that action would also be postponed with
regard to the Embassy's request of July 31 involving departure
from Japan of Shinki Tokashiki and other Japanese citizens whose
entry into Argentina had been authorized.
Through decisions made in the Division of Protective Services,
it is currently the policy of the Department to postpone action
on all similar requests of the Argentine Government until such
time as the matter of Argentine representation in Japan is made
clear by that Government.
CS

=== Page 216 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: August 27, 1948
Exthsion of the time during which Mr. Jakob
SUBJECT:
Szpiro may enter Japan.
PARTICIPAnTs: Mr. Iewin Spiro, President Globex Trading Co.,
New York.
Mrs. Mellor, DS
COPIES TO:
89
1-1408
Mr. Spiro called Mrs. Mellor this afternoon and stated that hi
representative Mr. Jakob Szpiro who had a military permit to enter
Japan between August 1 and August 31, 1948 was finding it difficult
27
to arrange his affairs so that he could reach Japan by August 31.
(Mr. Szpiro is presently in Shanghai and was notified there of his
48
Theater clearance). Mr. Spiro requested that his representative's
military per mit be amended so as to permit him to enter Japan at
a later date. Mrs. Mellor informed Mr. Spiro that the Department
of State does not grant permits to enter Japan; that it is solely
the jurisdiction of the Army Department; that the policy of the
Military Permit Section of tne Department of the Army is that any
one who has been granted a military permit to enter Japan and is
not able to use it within the allotted time must file a new
application. with the Regional Office of the Department of Commerce.
Er. Spiro then said, "in other words, it is a new case"; to which
Mrs. Mellor replied yes.
Mr. Spiro thanked Mrs. Mellor for the information.
C

lIcdE

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

September 7, 1948
MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES
RE:
Theater olearance to enter Japan for Milos George
Stejskal and Clga, his wife.
七
DS had no record of this application when the
memorandum from the Military Permit Section, Army
Department was received giving Theater clearance on
compassionate grounds. Mrs. Mellor called Major Larson
(MPS) and asked him to set the dates between which
8
they could enter Japan. Major Larson said when a
desired date of entry was not specified it was usual
to give the 60-day period.
Wnen the attached telegram (No. 1394 from Praha)
was received Mrs. Mellor again called Major Larson
and informed him the Embassy was requesting to be
informed during what period the permission was valid.
Major Larson said that when individuals requesting entry
8
into Japan were not traders it wasn't customary to set
the dates between which they could enter. However, he
3
stipulated the date for entry to be between September 15
and November 15 (60 days) which he thought would simplify
matters for the STEJSKAIS. I told Major Larson the Embassy
also requested that be informed of the point of entry and
in this regard quoted specific sections from SCAP Circular 8.
He said there were twelve points of entry but the usual
entry points were Tokyo and Kobe, and that they could enter
at any of the twelve points. Mrs. Mellor asked him if that
would be all right with SCAP and Major Larson said yes,
that at the point they entered notification would be sent
to SCAP Headquarters.
If the Department were to stipulate a point of entry
it mignt be quite a distance from the Stejskals desired
destination. Not naving any correspondence on the case
makes it difficult. I then asked Major Larson if it would
be all right to say in the telegram "entry point optional".
He said yes.
It is assumed that travel agencies do not sell tickets
for travel to Japan either by air or boat except to one of the
twelve entry points. The Stejskals should be able to find
fen
out in Praha what those entry points are and cnoose the one
which will be nearest to their desired destination.

-248
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Mrs. Mellor called INS and talked with Mrs. Gazzara regarding a re-
1-1608
entry permit for Mr. Leslie Cecil Dennis a British subject who has
received a military permit to go to Japan as a representative for the
Standard-Vacuum Cil Company and intends to stay three years.
Mrs. Gazzara stated that if Mr. Dennis desires to leave the US
without a re-entry permit he may do so, but when he desires to return
to the Us he will have to have enother visa. A re-entry permit is valid
for one year only but it may be renewed every six months, for which therg
is a cnarge of $3.00 each time. Mrs. Gazzara stated if Mr. Dennis were
absolutely certain he will stay three years, it would appear preferable
for him to get a visa when he desires to return to this country, which
he can do by applying to the nearest American representative. However, if
he would prefer to have the re-entry document in his possession (in
case a contingency arose whereby he might be called back to the US
4
within the year) he can get it. In that event he could get on the
ship to return without reference to any American Consul.
E
Mrs. Gazzara said further that a re-entry permit can be mailed in care
of an American representative abroad, but only if the application form has
been made out in this country by the interested person.
It would be a risky procedure to leave the Us without a re-entry
permit in many instances, but it is my understanding that the British
immigration quota is large and not more tnan 5% of it is used each year.

GENERAL MEIGS of the American President Lines, leaving San Fran-

The Acting Secretary of State refers to the Eabassy's despateh

no. 196l of September 24, 1948 tranamitting the application of
Walter Spitzer and his wife, Missi Spitzer, Austrian nationals. for
permission to enter Japan. It is stated in the application that
Ir. pitzer is eaployed by Max Koohmann, Limited, a British firm.
For the information of the Officer in Charge the Military
Permit Offioe at Washington was reluctant to present tho applioa-
tion under reference to the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
at Tokyo for consideration in view of the fact that r. Spitzer
will represent & Eritish firm in Japan. Established procodure
concerning the entry into Japan of business men representing British
firms provides that the applications of such individuals be presented
to the Supreme Comander through British channels, i.e., the British
CS/B
Board of Trede end the United Kingdom Lieison Misaion at Tokyo. With
respect to this partioular case, however, in the intorest of saving
time the Military Permit Office presented it to the Supreme Commander.
In this connection the Department is now informed by the Military
Permit Office that Theater Headquarters, Tokyo, requests thet this
application be presented through the British Board of Trade. It is
stated that the United Kingdom Liaison Mission at Tokyo has no
knowledge of the firm of Max Kochmann, Limited.
The Orficer in Charge is requested to communicate the pertinent
portion of this instruction to Mr. Spitzer.

ian passport.

Mr. Horrocks of the Legation of the Union of South
Africa called Mrs. Mellor this morning and stated that
Mr. Harry Julius Hurwitz, a national of the Union of South
Africa, who had been granted Theater clearance to enter
Japan between September 20 and October 20, 1948 has been
unable to utilize his clearance because of the shipping
strike on the West Coast. He asked if Mr. Hurwitz might
obtain an extension and lirs. Mellor said she would ask the

Military Permit Office. He desired the entry date to be

between November 10 and December 10, 1948. Mrs. Mellor
called liajor Larson of the MPo and he agreed to extend the
time as requested. Mrs. liellor informed Major Larson that
in as much as lir. Hurwitz is in the United States, Mr.
Hurwitz would like a military permit document. Major Larson
said he would send it Monday.
Mrs. Mellor called ir. Horrocks of the South African
Legation and gave him this information for which he expressed
his gratitude.

=== Page 423 === (confidence 0.99)
RESTRICTED
ENCLOSURE
TRAVEL OUTSIDE JAPAN CF JAPANESE COMM RCIAL REPRESENTATIVES
1. During the period of the occupation it is recognized that the
operation of the private trade of Japan with other countries must be
conducted largely through foreign nationals traveling or residing in
Japan. However, in order to widen the scope of trade and to further
the policy of preventing Japanese monopolies in foreign trade, a limited
resumption of private trade contacts by the travel of Japanese commercial
reprezentatives abroad may, subject to the approval of the country of
destination, be permitted under the following conditions:
a. Such travel should give Japanese no advantage over
foreign nationals doing business with or in Japan. To this
end foreign nationals in Japan should be given mavimum free-
dom to develop trade, subject only to the economic controls
required by reason of the occupation. Such controls should
aprly equally to Japanese and foreign nationals.
b. Japanese comnercial representatives abroad should not
be the sole sales or purchasing medium for Boeki Cho or for
Japanese trade associations nor should they be allowed to be
official renresentatives of CAF.
c. The specific travel authorized herein and the extent
thereof should be only that essential to raise Japan's foreign
trade to a level consistent with her peaceful needs as defined
by the Far Eastern Commission.
d. The activities of Japanese commercial representatives
abroad should be confined to trade. They should not be a
cover for other activities such as for instance those of a
-olitical or propeganda nature.
e. Japanese commercial representatives should be care-
fully cereened by the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
before they are allowed to leave Japan.
2. Luch Japanese travel abroad shall ce under such conditions and
controls as may be specified by the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers and the country of destination. Subject to over-all supervision
by the Suprome Co mander for the Allied Povers, the Japanese Government,
or any authorized agency thereof, will be responsible for the conduct of
any Japanese commercial representatives permitted to travel abroad under
this policy.

ly dear Mr. Secretary:
The Terms of Reference of the Far Eastern Commission provide
that one of the functions of the Commission should be to "formulate
the policies, principles and standards in conformity with which the
fulfillment by Japan of its obligations under the Terms of Surrender
10-2148
may be accomplished."
It is further provided that when such decisions are made by
the Far Eastern Commission, "The United States Government shall pre-
pare directives in accordance with the policy decisions of the Com-
mission and shall transmit them to the Supreme Commander through the
appropriate United States Government agency."
At the one hundred twenty-fourth meeting of the Far Eastern
Commission held at 2516 Massachusetts Avenue, Northwest, Washington,
D. C., on 21 October 19l8, the enclosed policy decision relative to
Travel Outside Japan of Japanese Commercial Representatives was ap-
proved. The Soviet Representative abstained from voting.
As Secretary General of the Far Eastern Commission, I have
been instructed to forward this decision to you on behalf of the Com-
mission in order that an appropriate directive may be prepared and
transmitted to the Supreme Commander in accordance with the Terms of
Reference.
At the time the enclosed policy was adopted several members
read into the minutes various statements of understanding as to the
meaning of certain portions. I am enclosing an excerpt from the draft
minutes of the one hundred twenty-fourth Commission meeting including
these statements for transmittal to the Supreme Conmander for his
information along with the policy decision itself.
The Commission agreed that the text of the enelosed policy
should be released to the press after the appropriate directive has
The Honorable Robert A. Lovett
Acting Secretary of State
Washington, D. C.
1.

arrangements for release as expeditiously as possible, it would be

has reached Tokyo.

peaceful needs es defined by the Far Eastern Commission.

should not be a cover for other setivities sueh as for

The Charge d'Affaires ad interim of Burma presents his compliments
to the Secretary of State and has the honour to refer to the note of
August 26, 1948 with which the Department of State returned to this
Embassy the applications of U Thein and U Hla Thwin, Managing Director
and Assistant Manager, respectively, of Maung Thein Brothers, Limited,
Rangoon, for permission to enter Japan with the intimation that for
the reasons given in the note the applications should be submitted to
the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers in Japan through British
894
channels.
The applications were accordingly forwarded to the Government
of the Union of Burma with a copy of the State Department's note.
The Foreign Office, Rangoon have, however, been informed by the
British Embassy, Rangoon that there has been no change in the procedure
by which applications by Burmese businessmen were to be submitted through
the State Department to the Supreme Commander and have returned the
applications of U Thein and U Hla Thwin for onward transmission to the
Supreme Commander through the State Department. A copy of letter
No. BW 998, dated October 9, 1948 from the Foreign Office, together
-2348
with a copy of letter, dated October 4, 1948 from the British Embassy,
Rangoon referred to therein, is attached herewith for information.
It is therefore requested that the enclosed applications of U
Thein and U Hla Thwin may be transmitted to the Supreme Commander for
consideration and the result intimated to this Embassy as soon as possible.
Washington, D. C.
Dated October 23, 1948.

FROM
MEMORANDUM FOR THEILESA
SUBJECT:
89
This morning, Thursday, November 4, Mr. Frank Loeb
called me on the telephone from New York and identified
himself as a Director of the Rio de Janeiro firm of
Gondrand and also a Director of the Brazilian travel
/11-448
tour organization, Brasiltur. Mr. Loeb stated that he
had received a telegram from Simon Fleiss in São Paulo.
The head of Brasiltur inquired as to when a reply might
be expected to the telegram which Fleiss sent me under
the date of October 22.
I outlined to Mr. Loeb the proper procedure for
the clearance of the projected Brasiltur tourist visit
to Japan of a large number of Japanese now resident in
Brazil. I told him that complete instructions had been
sent to the Consul General in São Paulo, Cecil Cross,
and I suggested to Mr. Loeb that he wire Fleiss to get
in touch with Mr. Cross, who could undoubtedly tell him
what the appropriate steps are to clear this tourist
trip. Mr. Loeb seemed very grateful for the informa-
tion and stated to me that he would wire Mr. Fleiss
immediately.

=== Page 493 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Despatch No. 724
dated November 12, 1948 from the
UNCLASSIFIED
United States Political Adviser
for Japen, Tokyo. on the subject
"Travel Abroad of Japanese Commer-
cial Representatives.
(COPY)
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
FAR EAST COMMAND
Public Infornation Office
1200
9 November 1948
ANNOUNCE POLICY TO PHRMIT JAPANESE BUSINESSMEN TO TRAVEL ABROAD
General Headquarters, SCAP, today announced that the Far
Eastern Commission has established a policy under the terms of
which Japanese nationals may travel outside of Japan for commer-
ciel purçoses.
In aoproving this policy on Cct. 21 the Far Eastern Com-
mission thus took another ster in raising the stringent restric-
tions which have been placed on travel of Japenese abroad.
This measure has been initiated in order to broaden the
scope of Janan's foreign trade. Its innlementation will insure
that Japanese businessmen do not gain an advantage over those
enjoyed by foreign naticnals who are engaged in business with
or in Japan.
The program will be administered by ScAP and all travel
under its terms will be subject to the approvel of the country
of destination. In addition, such travel will be only that
considered essential to raise Javan's trade to a level consist-
ent with her peaceful needs.
Japenese traveling abroad under this poliey may not engage
in political activities or propeganda but must confine them-
selves to trade.
All such travel is to be financially sponsored in full by
en individual, cency or organization which is non-Japanese in
character. Yen may not be converted to accomplish this sponsor-
ship. This type of travel will be authorized only when it is in
the overall interests of the private trade program for Japan.

To the
American Consular Officer in Charge,
Geneva, Switzerland.
The Acting Secretary of State refers to the Departmont's
telegram No. 1440 dated Uctober 21, 1948 instructing the Consulate
to notify the fira of Perret and Berthoud that the military authori-
ties in Japan had granted permission to their representative,
r. Edmond Laurence Bethell, a British national, to enter Japan
and inforaing the Consulate that the procedure followed in obtaining
Mr. Bethell's clearance was unusual and instructions would follow.
The Consular Ufficer in Charge is informed that the procedure
in connection with applications for permission to enter Japan for
trade purposes requires that the Government of the country in whieh
the firm is located certify the application of the firm's representa-
tive for permission to enter Japen and that it communicate the neces-
sary information in connection therewith to its Liaison Mission in
CS/A
Japan for presentation to the Supreme Commander. Therefore, despite
the fact that r. Bethell is a British national, his application
should have been processed by the Swiss authorities in Switserland
and the Swiss Liaison Mission at Tokyo.
A true copy of

My dear Mr. Myers:
Reference is made to your letter of November 23, 1948, whish
was addressed to the Passport Division, inquiring whether your
wife, who was born in Germany, could be given permission to eater
Japan should the posibility of your reentering the servise with
the Department of the Army in Japan materialise.
Upon recsipt of your letter this Division communicated with
the Military Permit Section, Department of the Army, and was informed
that your wife's German nationality would not prevent her from
obtaining a military permit for the purpose of accompanying you to
Japan.
1-2348
In this connection I should like to point out that unless
your wife has a travel document currently valid for travel to Japan
she should apply to the Interim Office for German Affairs of the
Department of State for a travel document in lieu of a passpert.
The Interim Office is authorized by law to issue travel doeuments
to German nationals in the United States, and in the case of your
wife the Interim Office would also apply for her military permit
to enter Japan.
Sincerely yours,
W.7e.

Dear Mr. Whitman:
I hope you still remember me, elthough we have not met since we
were in Minneapolis last April.
The Social Science Research Council has awarded me a fellowship
for a year's study of the Japanese economic recovery problem which we
were discussing at Minneapolis, but has offered no suggestions with
regard to gaining admission into Japan. I have been wondering whether
you or Mr. Bowman, to whom I am also writing, would have any suggestions
to offer regarding ways and means to obtain this vital permission prior
2-348
to January 1950, which is my deadline.
In particular, have you heard anything about general relaxation of
Generel MacArthur's present ban on "college professors" in Japan, to be
effective perhaps next Autumn? Again, would my chances be improved if
I could obtain a nominal (unpaid) status es Japanese correspondent for
a Madison or Milwaukee newspaper, in addition to my status as Research
Fellow? Are there any business firms which introduce people into Japan
x4745
as nominal representatives on terms which leave them free to devote
almost all their tims to research such as I have in mind?
Lt.Glshinn
With best personal regards,
C
Sincerely yours,

admission into Japan as to non-occupied foreign country,
through the Passport Division of the State Department, or is
there some special mechanism through either State or War?

Upon receipt of memorandum from the Military Permit Office,
Department of the Army, stating that SCAP had granted Theater clearance
for Mr. Zeki Keribar to enter Japan between December 15, 1948 and
January 15, 1949, Mrs. Mellor called the Embassy in view of the
Embassy's having submitted his application to the Department.
Mr. Keribar is in Hong Kong awaiting his Theater clearance.
Mrs. Mellor explained to Mr. Esenbel that in accordance with SCAP
Circular 8 of August 30, 1946 a statement would have to be placed
in Mr. Keribar's passport that he had been cleared by the Supreme
F2
Commander for entry into Japan. Mr. Esenbel stated that the Govern-
ment had an Embassy at Nanking but no representative at Hong Kong.
Mrs. Mellor suggested that the Department would send the notification
of Mr. Keribar's clearance to the American Consulate General at Hong
Kong, in view of the circumstances of the case, and that the American
Consulate General would be instructed to give Mr. Keribar a certified
copy of the cable notifying him of his clearance, in lieu of the
statement required by SCAP Circular 8.
6888
Mr. Esenbel said the Embassy would be grateful if the matter were
handled in this way. In view of the absence of a local address in
Hong Kong Mrs. Mellor inquired if the Consulate General would be able
to effect delivery of the cablegram. Mr. Esenbel said the Embassy
would send a message to Nanking to have it contact Mr. Keribar.
Mr. Esenbel stated further that he was sure Mr. Keribar was in touch
C
with the Consulate General as he was anxiously awaiting his Theater
C
f
clearance.

ScAP's press release of November 9 regarding trevel of commer-
cial representatives, which was forwarded with the reference des-
patch, was forehanded in that General Headquarters has not yet tak-
en any positive action toward allowing Japanese commercial repre-
sentatives to travel abroad in conformity with the Far Eastern Com-
mission': pclicy decision of October 21. 1948. A staff study, now
being completed in General Headquarters, will, it is reported, be
ready for implementation as from January 1, 1949.
The principal problem awaiting solution before general commer-
cial travel will be allowed concerns the means of financing such
travel. Although support may be provided by foreign firms and in-
dividuals, as has been done for educational and cultural travel, and
as was indicated in the anticipatory SCAP press release, the Econom-
ic and Scientific Section of General Headquarters is not satiefied
with that as a sole method of finance. It is therefore postponing
implementation of the policy until a program can be developed to
Aom
give the Japanese a share of the proceeds from their exports.
The plen et present under consideration would give Japenese ex
porters from 3 to 10 per cent of the returns from exports, depending
on the
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 570 === (confidence 0.98)
Tokyo' s Despatch No. 767
December 7, 1948
-2
on the commodity sold. Textiles would bring only the minimum of
3 per cent, while "miscellaneous goods," including almost everything
except textiles and metal products, would yield 10 per cent. The
foreign exchange thus acquired would be used both for travel and for
the payment of agents' commissions, and the program, if carried out,
should therefore answer questions raised both by foreign comercial
entrants and by foreign missions regarding SCAP's policy of allowing
payments of commissions only in yen. It should be noted, however,
thet foreign firms are not yet able to act in a general import-erport
capacity in Japan.
Pending formulation of a general policy, travel abroad of Japa-
nese commercial representatives will be allowed only in special cases.
Thus far, the only case déemed sufficiently preasing has been that of
the president of the Ajinomoto Company, who will go abroad to co-
ordinate wide sales of Ajinomoto condiment in the United States. His
trip to America, it has been decided, is directly in the interest of
the Occupation.
Respectfully yours,
levixcace
W. J. Sebald

The Department of State acknowledges the reseipt of the meme-
randum from the Legation of Lebanon dated December &, 1948 under
cover of whieh there was enclosed the application of Mr. Mishal J.
Chami, a citisen of Lobanon, for permission to enter Japan.
894

111/12-848
authorities and in reply the Department has received a communieation
stating that Mr. Chami may onter Japan between January 1 and
February 1, 1949 for a stay of sixty days after his date of entry.
The Department assumes that the Winistry of Foreign Affaire
will be requested to place in the passport of Mr. Chami a statement
to the effect that he has been cleared by the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers at Tokyo for entry into Japan. In this conneetion
there are enclosed two copies of a pamphlet prepared by the Supreme
Commander's Headquarters entitled Guide for Commereial Entrante to
